<div class="ra xbox p" role="complementary" aria-label="Related or updated information about this article"><div>The publisher's version of this article is available at <a href="https://doi.org/10.1126/sciimmunol.adu7961" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Sci Immunol</a>
</div></div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="ABS1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="P5">Germline-targeting is a promising approach to HIV vaccine development that begins with the elicitation of precursors to broadly neutralizing antibodies (bnAbs), but it remains unclear if simultaneous elicitation of precursors to multiple epitopes on the HIV Envelope (Env) would be inhibited by competition. This study used preclinical mouse models with physiologically relevant frequencies of bnAb-precursor-bearing B cells to compare precursor elicitation by co-administration of multiple protein or mRNA lipid nanoparticle (mRNA-LNP) germline-targeting immunogens. These immunogens activate multiple bnAb-precursor classes targeting distinct epitopes on Env, but with evidence of potential competition. Simultaneous delivery of immunogens encoded by mRNA-LNPs, however, drove maturation across different precursor frequencies and immunogen doses. Furthermore, administration of a cocktail of mRNA-LNP immunogens (N332-GT5 gp151, ApexGT5 gp151, eOD-GT8 60mer, and 10E8-GT12 24mer) led to balanced activation of four distinct bnAb-precursor classes, indicating that multi-epitope HIV bnAb-precursor priming might be successfully implemented in humans but might be immunogen dependent.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> V3-glycan, V2-apex, CD4bs, MPER, germline-targeting, HIV, mRNA-LNP, bnAbs, multiple priming</p></section></section><section id="S1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p id="P6">HIV remains incurable despite the remarkable impact of antiretroviral therapy (ART) (<a href="#R1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R1">1</a>–<a href="#R4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R4">4</a>), and approximately 1.7 million people were newly infected in 2019 alone (<a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">5</a>). The development of an effective vaccine, therefore, remains an urgent need. The Envelope (Env) spike protein is targeted by most broadly neutralizing antibodies (bnAb) from human HIV patients (<a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">6</a>, <a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">7</a>), making it the focal point for vaccine design. However, immunization with wild-type Env has not yet been proven to elicit bnAbs effectively, in part because the unmutated B cell receptors (BCRs) of B cells that may give rise to bnAb-producing descendants do not recognize the epitopes bound by their mature counterparts (<a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">8</a>). This gap between germline and mature bnAbs led to the development of germline targeting (GT) vaccine design (<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">9</a>–<a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">11</a>). The principle of GT vaccine design is to develop priming immunogens that activate naive B cells bearing germline BCRs that are potential bnAb precursors, which can then undergo somatic hypermutation (SHM) and diversification. Immunization with subsequent immunogens that more closely resemble native Env structures are used to shepherd this pool of activated B cell precursors along a mature-bnAb-like path (<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">9</a>, <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">12</a>–<a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">21</a>). GT immunogens have been developed to elicit different bnAb classes with distinct epitopes: VRC01-class bnAbs to the CD4 binding site (CD4bs) (<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">9</a>, <a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">10</a>, <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">12</a>, <a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">17</a>, <a href="#R18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R18">18</a>, <a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">22</a>, <a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">23</a>), BG18-class bnAbs to the V3-glycan (<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>, <a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">21</a>, <a href="#R24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R24">24</a>), PCT64-class and PG9-class bnAbs to the V2-apex epitope (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>–<a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">27</a>), and 10E8-class bnAbs to the membrane-proximal external region (MPER) (<a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">28</a>, <a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a>). These GT immunogens have demonstrated effective activation of germline precursors, laying the foundation for sequential immunization.</p>
<p id="P7">Unfortunately, the genetic diversity of HIV and the dense glycosylation of Env contribute to rapid neutralization escape (<a href="#R30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R30">30</a>–<a href="#R32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R32">32</a>) and pose an enormous challenge to Env-based vaccination. Some studies on passive administration of monoclonal antibodies (mAbs) suggest that cocktails of mAbs targeting different sites on Env may be more effective than single-mAb therapies, potentially limiting escape mutation routes (<a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">33</a>–<a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">39</a>). Effective vaccination may similarly require eliciting more than one class of bnAb targeting more than one site on Env. It is unknown, however, whether bnAb precursors to different epitopes can be primed simultaneously without competition that attenuates the responses, and the logic of GT design ensures that most GT priming immunogens generally elicit site-specific responses (<a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">40</a>). As multiple immunizations are already required for bnAb development (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a>, <a href="#R15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R15">15</a>), limiting total injections and healthcare visits by delivering a cocktail of immunogens with each vaccination will likely be key to uptake (<a href="#R41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R41">41</a>). Thus, the feasibility of GT immunogen coadministration is essential to the overall development of a GT vaccine for HIV.</p>
<p id="P8">Preclinical mouse models bearing inferred or genuine germline BCR sequences of human bnAbs provide an effective tool to evaluate pre-clinical vaccine candidates (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a>–<a href="#R15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R15">15</a>, <a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">20</a>, <a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">21</a>, <a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">23</a>, <a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>, <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">26</a>, <a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a>, <a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">42</a>–<a href="#R48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R48">48</a>). Previously, we established mouse models with either pre-rearranged heavy only or heavy and light chains from several classes of identified bnAb precursors (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>, <a href="#R49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R49">49</a>, <a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">50</a>). In this approach, B cells bearing knock-in BCRs are adoptively transferred from CD45.2 hosts to wild-type CD45.1 recipients to create stringent, low-frequency models. Here, we leveraged the adoptive transfer step to introduce different bnAb precursors targeting different epitopes on Env (CD4bs, V3-glycan, V2-apex, or MPER) into the same animal. We then simultaneously delivered two or more previously characterized GT priming immunogens and demonstrated that up to four precursor lineages can enter germinal centers (GCs), remain in those GCs for an extended period, and undergo SHM. This proof-of-principle for priming multiple bnAb classes provides critical insights toward the development of GT HIV vaccines.</p></section><section id="S2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results</h2>
<section id="S3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Co-administration of protein trimer immunogens targeting BG18-class/V3-glycan and PCT64-class/V2-apex precursors efficiently activate BG18-class precursors</h3>
<p id="P9">BnAbs to the V3-glycan/N332 high mannose patch epitope show encouraging neutralization (<a href="#R51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R51">51</a>–<a href="#R54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R54">54</a>); the most potent identified to date is BG18 (<a href="#R55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R55">55</a>). Previously, we established the mouse model BG18<sup>Hgl</sup>, where B cells express the BG18 inferred germline (iGL) heavy chain (HC) paired in BCRs with murine light chains (LCs) (<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>, <a href="#R49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R49">49</a>). The immunogen N332-GT5 gp151 (N332-GT5 for brevity) (<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>), which is now in a Phase 1 clinical trial (HVTN144; <a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/ct2/show/NCT06033209" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">NCT06033209</a>) based on strong performance in murine and non-human primate (NHP) models (<a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">21</a>, <a href="#R24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R24">24</a>), binds naïve BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> epitope-specific Fabs (mean K<sub>D</sub> = 9.3 nM) and can elicit responses in BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> as either a soluble protein trimer or a membrane-anchored protein encoded by mRNA-LNP (<a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">21</a>). The V2-apex-specific bnAb PCT64 is also an attractive vaccine target with substantial HC precursors in humans and relatively low rates of SHM required to reach maturity (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">26</a>, <a href="#R51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R51">51</a>, <a href="#R56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R56">56</a>–<a href="#R58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R58">58</a>). We previously developed a model bearing a least mutated common ancestor (LMCA)-reverted HC paired with either a human PCT64 germline LC or murine LCs, PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>). The trimer immunogen ApexGT5 gp151 (ApexGT5 for brevity) binds human PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> (K<sub>D</sub> = 66 nM) (<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">26</a>) and can activate PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> precursors in PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> as a soluble protein trimer adjuvanted with Sigma or when encoded as a membrane-anchored protein by mRNA-LNP (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>). Both N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 were designed around the BG505 SOSIP MD39 trimer (<a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a>), but with the addition of GT mutations to confer affinity for BG18-class precursors or PCT64- and PG9-class precursors, respectively (<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>, <a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">26</a>). To determine whether germline precursors to BG18 and PCT64 could be activated simultaneously using immunogens designed around the same stabilized trimer, we adoptively transferred B cells from both the BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> and PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> CD45.2 knockin mouse lines into congenic CD45.1 WT mice, establishing low, stringent frequencies within the ranges previously used for immunogen testing in these models (<a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">21</a>, <a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>). Co-transfer recipient mice were immunized intraperitoneally (IP) with both 5 μg of N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 soluble protein trimers formulated in Sigma adjuvant (Sigma, #S6322 SIGMA), and B cell responses were analyzed at days 14 and 28 post-prime (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1A</a>). Protein co-administration elicited robust GC reactions on day 14 (5.2%), which were diminished but still present on day 28 (2.0%) (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>, <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">C</a>). GCs contained CD45.2<sup>+</sup> precursor B cells at both time points (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>, <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">D</a>); however, this subpopulation consisted of N332-GT5 binders, rather than ApexGT5<sup>+</sup> cells, almost exclusively (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>, <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">B</a>, <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">E</a> and <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">F</a>). We examined the immunogen batch and confirmed that ApexGT5 protein eluted primarily as a single peak, exhibited a molecular weight within the anticipated range, and demonstrated strong binding affinity for PCT64 LMCA (K<sub>D</sub>=40 nM) (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S1</a>), suggesting that the weak PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> activation observed was not due to immunogen quality.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F1"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1. Limited co-activation of BG18-class/V3-glycan and PCT64-class/V2-apex bnAb precursors after dual delivery of BG505 SOSIP MD39-derived protein trimers.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12784461_nihms-2119667-f0001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ab94/12784461/98e7ded4612a/nihms-2119667-f0001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1050" width="923" alt="Figure 1."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P68">(<strong>A</strong>) Schematic showing naïve CD45.2<sup>+</sup> BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> (post-transfer frequency titrated to 7:10<sup>6</sup>) and PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> (post-transfer frequency = 10:10<sup>6</sup>) B cells adoptively transferred into CD45.1 WT mice (day −1); recipients were immunized with both N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 trimer protein via intraperitoneal (IP) injection. Samples were assayed 14 and 28 days post-immunization (dpi). Experiments were performed twice, and representative data from one are shown.</p>
<p id="P69">(<strong>B</strong>) Gating strategy plots showing germinal center (GC), CD45.2 cells in GC, N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 binders of CD45.2 cells in GC and binding specificity after immunization.</p>
<p id="P70">(<strong>C–F</strong>) Frequencies of GC B cells, CD45.2<sup>+</sup> B cell in GC, V3-glycan epitope specific (N332-GT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) and Apex epitope-specific (ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) binders of GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells gated as in (B), and CD45.2 epitope-specific binders in total B cells 14 and 28 dpi; n=5 in both time points. Statistics generated using Welch’s t test in (E &amp; F). Not detected (ND). Bars indicate mean + SD. *<em>P</em> &lt; 0.05; ****<em>P</em> &lt; 0.0001.</p>
<p id="P71">(<strong>G–J</strong>) Heavy chain nucleotide (nt) and amino acid (aa) mutations across all sites of BCRs isolated from CD45.2<sup>+</sup>Probe<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>IgD<sup>−</sup> B cells 14 and 28 dpi: gl BG18 HC nt (G); gl BG18 HC aa. Statistics generated using Mann-Whitney test in (G &amp; H). Bars indicate mean. ****<em>P</em> &lt; 0.0001. (H); LMCA PCT64 HC nt (I); LMCA PCT64 HC aa (J). LMCA PCT64 sequences were not recovered on day 14. Bars indicate mean.</p>
<p id="P72">(<strong>K–L</strong>) Per site heavy chain aa mutation frequency 28 dpi. CDRs boxed in grey; key mature (red) mutations were marked with letters; sequence number analyzed marked on the top left side. gl BG18 HC (K). LMCA PCT64 HC (L).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P10">Most known HIV bnAbs undergo a high number of mutations from germline to achieve affinity maturation (<a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">6</a>). Having established that BG18 and some PCT64 precursors entered GCs after co-administration, we investigated somatic hypermutation (SHM) in both lineages. We sorted CD45.2<sup>+</sup> antigen (Ag)-specific B cell binders and performed 10x BCR sequencing after dual trimer protein immunization (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S2A</a>). N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 WT probes were labelled with the same double fluoresceins; epitope knockout (KO) probes were labelled with a third fluorescein, after which we performed an unbiased probe-binding sort. In line with the flow cytometry results, 127 and 984 BG18 sequences were harvested at day 14 and 28, respectively; in contrast, only 17 PCT64 sequences were recovered at day 28 and none were recovered at day 14 (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>, <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">G</a> to <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">J</a>). The gl BG18 HCs accumulated both nucleotide (nt) and amino acid (aa) mutations over time (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>, <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">G</a> and <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">H</a>). Mutations of both gl BG18 and LMCA PCT64 HCs at day 28 were enriched in the HCDRs, which also displayed key mature (G27D in BG18; N31D and K52R in PCT64) mutations (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a>, <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">K</a> and <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">L</a>). In sum, while BG18-class/V3-glycan and PCT64-class/V2-apex bnAb precursors were both activated by coadministration of N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 trimer protein, leading to SHM in both lineages, the ApexGT5 response in GCs was weak.</p></section><section id="S4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Increased precursor frequencies enhance PCT64-class/V2-apex responses after dual protein trimer immunization</h3>
<p id="P11">Precursor frequency can be a major determinant of the magnitude and quality of the response to immunogens (<a href="#R59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R59">59</a>). To investigate whether the limited PCT64-class/V2-apex response to protein could be rescued by altering precursor frequencies, we adoptively transferred CD45.2 BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> and PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> B cells at variable starting frequencies into the same murine hosts. BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> was titrated to a frequency of either 7:10<sup>6</sup> (referred to as 1x) or 70:10<sup>6</sup> (10x), PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> at 10:10<sup>6</sup> (1x) or 100:10<sup>6</sup> (10x), to produce models with either a high starting BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> B cell frequency, BG18<sup>High</sup> (BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> 10x:PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> 1x), or a high PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> B cell frequency, PCT64<sup>High</sup> (BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> 1x:PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> 10x). We then primed the recipients with both N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 trimer protein in Sigma adjuvant and collected samples 14 and 28 dpi for analysis (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2A</a>). Dual immunization induced similar GC sizes and CD45.2 cell participation in GCs at both frequencies (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>, <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">B</a> to <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">D</a>). The GC CD45.2 population primarily consisted of N332-GT5 binders no matter the initial precursor frequency; however, the PCT64<sup>High</sup> condition did allow for increased ApexGT5<sup>+</sup> CD45.2 B cell participation in GCs in several mice (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>, <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">E</a>) and a significantly higher proportion of CD45.2<sup>+</sup> ApexGT5 binders in total B cells at day 14 (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2F</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F2"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2. Increased precursor frequency improves dual activation of PCT64-class/V2-apex and BG18-class/V3-glycan bnAb precursors by protein trimers.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12784461_nihms-2119667-f0002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ab94/12784461/33e641cd0a80/nihms-2119667-f0002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1050" width="776" alt="Figure 2."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P73">(<strong>A</strong>) Schematic showing naïve CD45.2 BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> and PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> B cells adoptively transferred to CD45.1 WT mice (day −1) to establish distinct starting precursor frequency ratios. “1/1X” post-transfer frequencies for BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> and PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> were as in prior experiments (approximately 7:10<sup>6</sup> and 10:10<sup>6</sup>, respectively) while “10/10X” indicates respective post-transfer frequencies of 70:10<sup>6</sup> and 100:10<sup>6</sup>. Recipients were immunized IP with both N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 trimer protein (day 0). Samples were collected 14 and 28 dpi for analysis. Data was collected from a single run.</p>
<p id="P74">(<strong>B</strong>) Gating strategy plots showing GC, CD45.2 cells in GC, N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 binders of CD45.2 cells in GC and binding specificity after immunization.</p>
<p id="P75">(<strong>C–F</strong>) Frequencies of GC B cells, CD45.2<sup>+</sup> B cell in GC, V3-glycan epitope specific (N332-GT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) and Apex epitope-specific (ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) binders of GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells gated as in (B), and CD45.2 epitope-specific binders in total B cells at 14 and 28 dpi; n=5 in each condition. Statistics generated using One-Way ANOVA analysis with Tukey’s multiple comparison test in (C), Brown-Forsythe and Welch ANOVA with Dunnett’s T3 multiple comparison (D), Welch’s or Unpaired t test in (E &amp; F). Not detected (ND). Bars indicate mean + SD. Not significant (ns); *<em>P</em> &lt; 0.05.</p>
<p id="P76">(<strong>G–J</strong>) Heavy chain nucleotide (nt) and amino acid (aa) mutations across all sites of BCRs isolated from CD45.2<sup>+</sup>Probe<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>IgD<sup>−</sup> B cells 14 and 28 dpi. gl BG18 HC nt (G); gl BG18 HC aa (H); LMCA PCT64 HC nt (I); LMCA PCT64 HC aa (J). Statistics generated using Kruskal-Wallis test. Bars indicate mean. Not significant (ns). Note: LMCA PCT64 sequences in BG18<sup>High</sup> frequency group were not recovered on day 14.</p>
<p id="P77">(<strong>K–L</strong>) Per site heavy chain aa mutation frequency at 28 dpi. CDRs boxed in grey; key mature (red) and on-track (dark blue) mutations for PCT64 were marked with letters (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>, <a href="#R56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R56">56</a>); sequence number analyzed marked on the top left side. gl BG18 HC: BG18<sup>High</sup> (top); PCT64<sup>High</sup> (bottom) (K). LMCA PCT64 HC: BG18<sup>High</sup> (top); PCT64<sup>High</sup> (bottom) (L).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P12">To determine whether altered starting frequencies affected SHM for either gl BG18 or LMCA PCT64, CD45.2<sup>+</sup> epitope-specific binders were sorted and single-cell BCR sequencing was performed as above. Both gl BG18 and LMCA PCT64 HCs accumulated mutations over time, although LMCA PCT64 sequences were not recovered at day 14 in the BG18<sup>High</sup> treatment. Differential precursor frequency led to no significant differences in gl BG18 or LMCA PCT64 mutations, though the absence of LMCA PCT64 sequences in the BG18<sup>High</sup> condition at day 14 and the low number at day 28 limited the conclusiveness of this observation (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a>, <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">G</a> to <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">J</a>). Gl BG18 HC hotspot mutation patterns were almost identical regardless of initial precursor frequency (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2K</a>). LMCA PCT64 HC mutation patterns were more variable, likely due to limited sequence recovery in the BG18<sup>High</sup> condition, but they exhibited similar key mature mutations as well as intermediate “on-track” mutations identified from the PCT64 lineage (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2L</a>) (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>, <a href="#R56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R56">56</a>). In sum, increased starting frequencies improved the participation of PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> B cells in the GC after dual protein immunization and allowed both lineages to undergo SHM.</p></section><section id="S5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">An mRNA-LNP cocktail activates both BG18-class/V3-glycan and PCT64-class/V2-apex bnAb precursors efficiently</h3>
<p id="P13">While the number of potential precursors can be increased by GT mutations during the design of the priming immunogen (<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>), it is not feasible to increase the starting precursor frequency to enhance responses to an existing immunogen in humans. We previously demonstrated that the membrane-anchored ApexGT5 encoded by mRNA-LNP lowers the affinity threshold required to activate PCT64 precursors relative to soluble ApexGT5 trimer protein (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>). To determine whether this held true in a dual vaccination regimen, CD45.1 WT recipients were co-transferred with CD45.2 BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> and PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> cells and then immunized intramuscularly (IM) with mRNA-LNPs encoding membrane-anchored trimers of either N332-GT5, ApexGT5, or both at a dose of 3.8 μg each and assayed 14 and 28 dpi (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3A</a>). There was no significant difference in GC sizes between single or dual immunization at day 14 or 28 (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>, <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">C</a>). N332-GT5 alone recruited more CD45.2 cells to GCs than ApexGT5 alone at day 14, but the two were indistinguishable at day 28; CD45.2 as a fraction of GCs at day 28 was highest after dual administration (~40%), but this was significant only in comparison to N332-GT5 alone (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3D</a>). As a fraction of total B cells, GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> N332-GT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup> and ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup> were not negatively affected by dual relative to single delivery of their respective immunogens; ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup> cells seemed to be somewhat enhanced, but this was not significant (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3E</a>). Serum IgG levels against N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 were also similar after single and dual immunizations (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>, <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">F</a> and <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">G</a>, and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S3</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">A</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">B</a>). gl BG18 HCs acquired nt and aa mutations at the same frequency after single or dual immunization (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>, <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">H</a> and <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">I</a>). LMCA PCT64 HCs, however, displayed more mutations at day 28 after dual immunization than after ApexGT5 alone (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a>, <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">J</a> and <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">K</a>). Hotspot mutations in both gl BG18 and LMCA PCT64 HCs developed in similar HCDR locations between dual immunization and their respective single immunizations, though the mutation rate at the G110 site in LMCA PCT64 was higher after dual than single ApexGT5 immunization (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S3</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">C</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">D</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F3"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3. An mRNA-LNP cocktail efficiently activates both PCT64-class/V2-apex and BG18-class/ V3-glycan bnAb precursors.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12784461_nihms-2119667-f0003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ab94/12784461/62ba798726b4/nihms-2119667-f0003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1050" width="1017" alt="Figure 3."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P78">(<strong>A</strong>) Schematic showing naïve CD45.2 BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> (post-transfer frequency = 7:10<sup>6</sup>) and PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> (post-transfer frequency = 10:10<sup>6</sup>) B cells adoptively transferred into CD45.1 WT recipients (day −1), which were then immunized with either single N332-GT5, ApexGT5 trimer Mrna-LNP, or both via intramuscular (IM) injection (day 0). Samples were collected at 14 and 28 dpi. Experiments were performed twice, and representative data from one experiment are shown.</p>
<p id="P79">(<strong>B</strong>) Gating strategy plots showing GC, CD45.2 cells in GC, N332-GT5- and ApexGT5-binders among GC CD45.2 cells and binding specificity after immunization.</p>
<p id="P80">(<strong>C–E</strong>) Frequencies of GC B cells (C), CD45.2<sup>+</sup> B cells in GC (D), V3-glycan-specific (N332-GT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) and Apex-specific (ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells in total B cells in each condition at 14 and 28 dpi (E). Each symbol represents a different mouse, n=5–6 in each condition. Statistics generated using Brown-Forsythe and Welch ANOVA with Dunnett’s T3 multiple comparison (C), One-Way ANOVA analysis with Tukey’s multiple comparison test in (D), Mann-Whitney or Unpaired t test in (E). Not detected (ND). Bars indicate mean + SD. Not significant (ns); *<em>P</em> &lt; 0.05; **<em>P</em> &lt; 0.01.</p>
<p id="P81">(<strong>F–G</strong>) Serum IgG antibody titer measurement. ΔAUC (delta area under the curve) values were compared for N332-GT5 and N332-GT5 KO titers (F) or ApexGT5 and ApexGT5 KO titers (G). Each symbol represents a different mouse with 5–6 mice at each time point. Bars represent mean + SD. Statistical analysis was performed using One-Way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple comparison test. Bars indicate mean + SD. Not significant (ns).</p>
<p id="P82">(<strong>H–K</strong>) Number of heavy chain nt and aa mutations across all sites of BCRs isolated from CD45.2<sup>+</sup>Probe<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>IgD<sup>−</sup> B cells at 14 and 28 dpi. gl BG18 HC nt (H); gl BG18 HC aa (I); LMCA PCT64 HC nt (J); LMCA PCT64 HC aa (K). Statistical analysis was performed using Kruskal-Wallis test. Bars indicate mean. Not significant (ns); ****<em>P</em> &lt; 0.0001.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P14">To determine whether the improved Apex response might be driven by the membrane-bound presentation of mRNA-LNP ApexGT5 or, alternatively, other effects of mRNA-LNP formulation, we transferred PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> cells and subsequently primed the mice with mRNA-LNP encoding ApexGT6 (<a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">27</a>), a related ApexGT priming immunogen, in either a membrane-bound or soluble format (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S4A</a>). The membrane-bound mRNA induced larger GC sizes 10 dpi (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S4</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">C</a>) and recruited more CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells for activation and expansion (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S4</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">D</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">F</a>).</p>
<p id="P15">In sum, a cocktail of N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 membrane-bound trimer mRNA-LNPs improved the activation of PCT64 LMCA precursors relative to soluble protein co-administration and allowed them to undergo SHM. Membrane-bound antigen presentation may underly this difference by lowering the precursor activation threshold.</p></section><section id="S6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">BG18-class/V3-glycan and PCT64-class/V2-apex respond to mRNA-LNP cocktails when potential competitors are abundant</h3>
<p id="P16">To determine whether the responses to the N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 mRNA-LNP cocktail were similarly affected by precursor frequency, we again established BG18<sup>High</sup> and PCT64<sup>High</sup> models. We primed the recipients with both N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 mRNA-LNPs and collected samples 14 and 28 dpi for analysis (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4A</a>). Dual immunization induced similar GC sizes at both frequencies (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>, <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">C</a>), and CD45.2 B cell occupation of GCs was similar (~40%) in both cohorts at days 14 and 28 (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4D</a>). Higher starting frequencies generally increased Probe<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup> B cells targeting corresponding epitopes as a fraction of either GC CD45.2 B cells or total B cells, but these increases were not significant (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>, <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">E</a> and <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">F</a>). Low BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> starting frequency had no effect on the serum IgG levels of N332-GT5 antibodies (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>, <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">G</a> and <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">H</a>), and, though ApexGT5 ED<sub>50</sub> was lower at day 14 in the low PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> transfer model, this gap equilibrated by day 28, and epitope-specific ApexGT5 antibodies were indistinguishable at both time points (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a>, <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">I</a> and <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">J</a>). Changing the starting frequencies within a 10-fold range therefore had minimal impact on the response.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F4"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4. Simultaneous activation of BG18-class/V3-glycan and PCT64-class/V2-apex-responses by mRNA-LNP immunization at variable precursor frequencies.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12784461_nihms-2119667-f0004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ab94/12784461/150d5b95ad49/nihms-2119667-f0004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1050" width="930" alt="Figure 4."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>

Recipients were then immunized with both N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 trimer mRNA-LNPs via IM injection (day 0). Samples were collected 14 and 28 dpi. Experiments were performed twice, and representative data from one experiment are shown.</p>

<p id="P85">(<strong>C–F</strong>) Frequencies of GC B cells (C), CD45.2<sup>+</sup> B cell in GC (D), V3-glycan epitope specific (N332-GT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) and Apex epitope specific (ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) binders among GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells (E), and CD45.2 epitope-specific binders in total B cells (F) 14 and 28 dpi; n=5–6 in each condition. ΔAUC comparison for N332-GT5 and N332-GT5 KO titer (H), ApexGT5 and ApexGT5 KO titer (J). Each symbol represents a different mouse with 5–6 mice in each condition. Bars represent geometric mean + geometric SD (G &amp; I) or indicate mean + SD (H &amp; J). Statistics generated using Brown-Forsythe and Welch’s ANOVA with Dunnett’s T3 multiple comparison (G) and One-Way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple comparison test in (H–J). Not significant (ns); *<em>P</em> &lt; 0.05.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P17">To determine whether starting frequency alterations affected SHM, CD45.2<sup>+</sup> epitope-specific binders were sorted and single-cell BCR sequencing was performed as above. Both gl BG18 and LMCA PCT64 HCs accumulated mutations over time, and there were no significant differences in total nt or aa mutations of gl BG18 related to starting frequency; although slightly more aa (day 14) and nt (day 28) mutations were observed in LMCA PCT64 HCs when PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> starting frequencies were higher (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S5</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">A</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">D</a>). Mutation patterns were also convergent regardless of initial precursor frequency (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S5</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">E</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">F</a>). Thus, while a 10-fold change in precursor frequency could improve V2-Apex responses to protein immunization, the mRNA-LNP format were not sensitive to precursor frequency changes within this range.</p></section><section id="S7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">VRC01-class bnAb precursors to the CD4bs can be activated in tandem with PCT64-class/V2-apex or BG18-class/V3-glycan bnAb precursors</h3>
<p id="P18">A number of GT immunogens are designed around VRC01-class bnAbs targeting the CD4bs (<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">9</a>, <a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">12</a>, <a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">17</a>, <a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">22</a>, <a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">23</a>, <a href="#R60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R60">60</a>), including an engineered outer domain of HIV gp120 (eOD-GT8) presented as 60 copies on a self-assembling soluble nanoparticle (60mer NP) (<a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">23</a>, <a href="#R61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R61">61</a>). Previously, we established the CLK family of mouse models (<a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">50</a>), wherein B cells express germline knockin HC and LCs of VRC01 bnAb precursors (<a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">62</a>) which are efficiently activated by the eOD-GT8 60mer NP (<a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">50</a>). To determine whether VRC01-class precursors could participate in multi-site activation responses after the co-administration of protein trimer and NP immunogens, we adoptively transferred precursor B cells from PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> and CLK09 (which binds eOD-GT8 at K<sub>D</sub> = 350 nM (<a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">62</a>)) CD45.2 knockin mouse lines into congenic CD45.1 WT mice (<a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>, <a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">50</a>) at physiologically relevant frequencies previously used for this immunogen and model combination (<a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">20</a>, <a href="#R25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R25">25</a>) and immunized recipients with both 5 μg of ApexGT5 soluble protein trimer and 10 μg eOD-GT8 soluble 60mer with Sigma adjuvants. B cell responses were analyzed at days 14 and 28 post-prime (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5A</a>). Co-immunization with the protein immunogens induced robust GC responses with CD45.2 cells at both time points (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>, <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">B</a> to <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">D</a>), the vast majority of which were probe-positive for eOD-GT8 rather than ApexGT5 (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>, <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">E</a> and <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">F</a>). PCT64 and CLK09 HCs accumulated both nt and aa mutations over time, but, as expected from the flow cytometry results, while 539 and 1104 CLK09 sequences were isolated at day 14 and 28, respectively, only 6 and 42 PCT64 sequences were recovered on those time points (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S6</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">A</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">D</a>). Mutations at day 28 were enriched in the HCDRs, which also displayed on-track (S35N, K52E, and G110A in PCT64) or key mature (N31D, S35T, and G110D in PCT64; G31D/A in CLK09) mutations (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S6</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">E</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">F</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F5"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5. PCT64-class/V2-apex and VRC01-class/CD4bs bnAb precursors can be activated simultaneously with a trimer and nanoparticle immunogen.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12784461_nihms-2119667-f0005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ab94/12784461/bbf6ad4d932b/nihms-2119667-f0005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1050" width="762" alt="Figure 5."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P87">(<strong>A</strong>) Schematic showing naïve CD45.2 PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> (post-transfer frequency = 10:10<sup>6</sup>) and CLK09 (post-transfer frequency = 20:10<sup>6</sup>) B cells adoptively transferred into CD45.1 WT mice (day −1); recipients were immunized with both ApexGT5 trimer and eOD-GT8 60mer protein via IP injection. Samples were assayed at 14 and 28 dpi. Data collected from a single run.</p>
<p id="P88">(<strong>B</strong>) Gating strategy plots showing GC, CD45.2 cells in GC, ApexGT5 and eOD-GT8 binders of CD45.2 cells in GC, and epitope binding specificity after protein coadministration.</p>
<p id="P89">(<strong>C–F</strong>) Frequencies of GC B cells (C), CD45.2<sup>+</sup> B cell in GC (D), Apex epitope-specific (ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) and CD4bs-epitope-specific (eOD-GT8<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) binders of GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells (E) gated as in (B) and CD45.2<sup>+</sup> epitope specific binders in total B cells (F) at 14 and 28 dpi; n=5 in both time points. Statistics generated using Brown-Forsythe and Welch’s ANOVA with Dunnett’s T3 multiple comparison in (E, F). Not detected (ND). Bars indicate mean + SD. Not significant (ns); **<em>P</em> &lt; 0.01; ***<em>P</em> &lt; 0.001.</p>
<p id="P90">(<strong>G</strong>) Schematic showing naïve CD45.2 PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> (post-transfer frequency = 10:10<sup>6</sup>) and CLK09 (post-transfer frequency = 20:10<sup>6</sup>) B cell adoptive transfer recipients immunized with both ApexGT5 trimer and eOD-GT8 60mer mRNA-LNPs via IM injection. Samples were collected at 14 and 28 dpi. Data collected from a single run.</p>
<p id="P91">(<strong>H</strong>) Gating strategy plots showing GC, CD45.2 cells in GC, ApexGT5 and eOD-GT8 binders of CD45.2 cells in GC and epitope binding specificity after administration of mRNA-LNP cocktail.</p>
<p id="P92">(<strong>I–L</strong>) Frequencies of GC B cells (I), CD45.2<sup>+</sup> B cell in GC (J), Apex epitope specific (ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) and CD4bs epitope specific (eOD-GT8<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) binders of GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells (K) gated as in (H) and CD45.2<sup>+</sup> epitope specific binders in total B cells (L) 14 and 28 dpi; n=5 in both time points. Statistics generated using One-Way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple comparison test in (K) and Brown-Forsythe and Welch’s ANOVA with Dunnett’s T3 multiple comparison (L). Bars indicate mean + SD. Not significant (ns).</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P19">eOD-GT8 60mer is also highly immunogenic when delivered by mRNA-LNP as a self-assembling soluble NP (<a href="#R19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R19">19</a>, <a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">20</a>). To determine whether ApexGT5 mRNA-LNP could again improve PCT64-class/V2-Apex precursor activation as part of an mRNA-LNP cocktail, we established a dual transfer model as above and immunized the recipients with a membrane-anchored ApexGT5 trimer and a soluble eOD-GT8 60mer mRNA-LNP (dose: 3.8 μg each) and assayed responses at the same time points (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5G</a>). Similar to protein immunogens, co-delivery of both mRNA-LNPs elicited strong GC reactions on day 14, which remained, though diminished, until day 28 (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>, <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">H</a> and <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">I</a>). The GCs contained ~8% CD45.2 B cells at both time points (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>, <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">H</a> and <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">J</a>). In contrast to protein immunization, the GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> bnAb precursor repertoire consisted of 24.0% ApexGT5- and 41.1% eOD-GT8- specific binders on day 14, equalizing to ~30% on day 28 (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a>, <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">H</a> and <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">K</a>). The proportion of both precursors was similar as a percentage of total B cells at day 28 (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5L</a>). Co-immunization elicited serum IgG titer against both epitopes (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S6</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">G</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">I</a>), and ΔAUC specific to each epitope were high at day 14 but waned at day 28 (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S6</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">H</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">J</a>). HCs from both PCT64 and CLK09 gained SHM over time after dual mRNA-LNP (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S6</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">K</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">N</a>), exhibiting similar mutation patterns and on-track or key mature mutations (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S6</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">E</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">F</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">O</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">P</a>). Thus, while PCT64-class/V2-apex and VRC01-class/CD4bs bnAb precursors were activated simultaneously and underwent SHM after co-administration of ApexGT5 trimer and eOD-GT8 60mer, the ApexGT5 response in GCs was limited unless the immunogens were delivered as mRNA-LNPs.</p>
<p id="P20">To determine whether mRNA-LNP eOD-GT8 60mer could also drive efficient activation with the N332-GT5 mRNA-LNP trimer immunogen, we adoptively transferred BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> and CLK09 precursors into a single host and immunized recipients with mRNA-LNPs encoding either N332-GT5 trimer, eOD-GT8 60mer or both at a dose of 10 μg each and assayed 14 and 28 dpi (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S7A</a>). mRNA-LNP eOD-GT8 60mer on its own produced larger GCs than the N332-GT5 trimer mRNA-LNP, but co-delivery produced the largest GCs at both time points (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S7</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">C</a>). The proportion of CD45.2 cells recruited to GCs after single N332-GT5 trimer immunization was higher than after eOD-GT8 60mer or dual delivery (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S7D</a>). GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup>N332-GT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup> were, as a fraction of total B cells, statistically indistinguishable between single N332-GT5 and dual immunization; while the same was not true for CD45.2<sup>+</sup> eOD-GT8<sup>+</sup> KO<sup>−</sup> B cells at day 14, their numbers recovered by day 28 (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S7E</a>). Overall serum titers and eOD-GT8 CD4bs epitope-specific IgG levels were similar across treatments (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S7</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">F</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">H</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">I</a>), though serum N332-GT5 V3-glycan epitope-specific IgG levels did vary between single N332-GT5 immunization and dual immunization, but in opposing directions at days 14 and 28 (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S7G</a>). In terms of total SHM, dual immunization was either neutral or beneficial (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S8</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">A</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">D</a>), and the same key mature mutations and similar hotspot mutation patterns emerged in BG18 and CLK09 HCs (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S8</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">E</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">F</a>). In sum, precursors to two distinct bnAb classes could be activated and undergo SHM after immunization by mRNA-LNP cocktails containing eOD-GT8 60mer and either trimer.</p></section><section id="S8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Germline precursors to bnAbs targeting three distinct epitopes can be activated simultaneously</h3>
<p id="P21">Having interrogated two-way interactions between precursor classes, we advanced to triple activation. We first examined triple activation in CD45.1 WT mice adoptively transferred with each precursor after protein immunization and found that, as after dual immunization with protein, ApexGT5 responses were present but weak (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S9</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">A</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">F</a>). BG18 and CLK09 precursor HCs accumulated nt/aa mutations over time (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S9</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">G</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">H</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">K</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">L</a>); however, only two LMCA PCT64 sequences were recovered (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S9</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">I</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">J</a>). We therefore moved to immunization with a cocktail of all three mRNA-LNP immunogens (N332-GT5 trimer, ApexGT5 trimer and eOD-GT8 60mer) IM at a low dose (0.5 μg of each immunogen), an intermediate dose (2 μg each), or a high dose (10 μg each); responses were assayed at 14 and 28 dpi (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6A</a>). Higher doses generally increased total GC size (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a>, <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">B</a> and <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">C</a>), but total CD45.2 B cells in GCs were not affected by dose on average and displayed high variability (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6D</a>). Binders to all three probes were found in CD45.2 GC B cells (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6E</a>) and total CD45.2 B cells (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6F</a>), with significant differences appearing only in total B cells and on day 28 for N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 binders, with a substantial variability across individual mice (<a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6F</a>). Endogenous CD45.1 B cell responses in the mixed group were primarily against eOD-GT8 and did not vary significantly with dose (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S10</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">A</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">C</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F6"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6. BG18-class/V3-glycan, PCT64-class/V2-apex, and VRC01-class/CD4bs bnAb precursor responses can be elicited simultaneously.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12784461_nihms-2119667-f0006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ab94/12784461/e3c26f5673a9/nihms-2119667-f0006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="951" width="1050" alt="Figure 6."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P93">(<strong>A</strong>) Schematic showing naïve CD45.2 BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> (post-transfer frequency = 7:10<sup>6</sup>), PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> (post-transfer frequency = 10:10<sup>6</sup>), and CLK09 (post-transfer frequency = 20:10<sup>6</sup>) B cell adoptive transfer recipients immunized with triple mRNA-LNP immunogens (N332-GT5 trimer, ApexGT5 trimer, and eOD-GT8 60mer) simultaneously via IM injection. Samples were collected at 14 and 28 dpi. Data were pooled and shown from two experiments (n=10–12).</p>
<p id="P94">(<strong>B</strong>) Gating strategy plots showing GC, CD45.2 cells in GC, N332-GT5, ApexGT5 and eOD-GT8 epitope-specific binders among GC CD45.2 cells after immunization.</p>
<p id="P95">(<strong>C–F</strong>) Frequencies of GC B cells (C), CD45.2<sup>+</sup> B cells in GC (D), V3-glycan epitope specific (N332-GT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>), Apex-specific (ApexGT5<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) and CD4bs-specific (eOD-GT8<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>) binders of GC CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells (E) gated as in (B), and CD45.2 epitope specific binders in total B cells (F) at 14 and 28 dpi; n=10–12 in each condition. Statistics generated using Brown-Forsythe and Welch’s ANOVA with Dunnett’s T3 multiple comparison (C &amp; E), One-Way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple comparison and t test (D &amp; F). Not detected (ND). Bars indicate mean + SD. Not significant (ns); *<em>P</em> &lt; 0.05; **<em>P</em> &lt; 0.01; ***<em>P</em> &lt; 0.001.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P22">In terms of antibody production, total WT-antigen and epitope-KO-antigen IgG AUC generally increased with dose (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S11</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">A</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">F</a>), but ΔAUC was similar across doses (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>, <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">A</a> to <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">C</a>). While otherwise dose-independent, epitope-specific CD45.2 plasmablast generation on day 28 did significantly increase for N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 at higher doses; CD45.1 epitope-specific plasmablasts either did not vary or, on day 28 for ApexGT5, decreased (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S10</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">D</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">E</a>). Electron microscopy polyclonal epitope mapping (EMPEM) was used to assess whether serum antibodies against the V3-glycan and V2-apex epitopes were detectable after priming with the mRNA-LNP cocktail or protein cocktail. Polyclonal Fabs isolated from the sera of each study group were incubated with a cocktail of N332-GT5, ApexGT5, and eOD-GT8. As the mass of eOD-GT8 is too low for EMPEM, and the NP version too flexible for accurate alignment of particles by EM, it was excluded from downstream EMPEM analysis. EM 3D reconstructions revealed responses against the V3-glycan epitope in both study groups (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>, <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">D</a> and <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">E</a>), and additional responses to the gp41-base in the protein-only group. These EM maps have high overlap with published EMPEM maps obtained from single immunogen (N332-GT5) priming (<a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">21</a>), confirming that the desired V3-glycan response can be elicited even in the presence of competing immunogens. V2-apex polyclonal antibodies were not detected, which correlated with the lower titers compared to V3-glycan antibodies and/or lower affinities of such antibodies at the day 28 time point.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F7"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7. BG18<sup>Hgl</sup>, PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup>, and CLK09 produce serum antibody and undergo SHM during simultaneous activation.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12784461_nihms-2119667-f0007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ab94/12784461/faa3719c06de/nihms-2119667-f0007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1050" width="811" alt="Figure 7."></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P96">(<strong>A–C</strong>) ELISA quantification of N332-GT5-specific, ApexGT5-specific and eOD-GT8-specific IgG at 14 and 28 dpi. ΔAUC comparison of titers for N332-GT5 and N332-GT5-KO (A); ApexGT5 and ApexGT5-KO (B); eOD-GT8 and eOD-GT8-KO (C). Each symbol represents a different mouse with 5–6 mice in each condition. Statistics generated using One-Way ANOVA with Tukey’s multiple comparison test. Bars indicate mean + SD. Not significant (ns); *<em>P</em> &lt; 0.05.</p>
<p id="P97">(<strong>D-E</strong>) Electron microscopy polyclonal epitope mapping (EMPEM) following multi-priming immunization. A cocktail of N332-GT5 and ApexGT5 was used to form complexes with pooled serum IgG (digested to Fab) from triple mRNA-LNPs (high dose, panel D) or proteins (5μg N332-GT5, 5μg ApexGT5, 10μg eOD-GT8, panel E) primed mice at day 28. Responses against the V3-glycan epitope were detected in both study groups (green segment). An additional response against the gp41-base was detected in the protein group (purple segment). When compared to published EMPEM maps following priming with a single immunogen (N332-GT5), the angles of approach and binding positions of the V3-glycan responses are highly overlapping, even after simultaneous priming against additional epitopes.</p>
<p id="P98">(<strong>F–H</strong>) Mutations across all sites of BCR heavy chains sequenced from CD45.2<sup>+</sup>Probe<sup>+</sup>KO<sup>−</sup>IgD<sup>−</sup> B cells 14 and 28 dpi. gl BG18 HC aa (F); LMCA PCT64 HC aa (G); CLK09 HC aa (H). Statistical analysis performed using Kruskal-Wallis test. Bars indicate mean. Not significant (ns); **<em>P</em> &lt; 0.01.</p>
<p id="P99">(<strong>I</strong>) Per site heavy chain aa mutation frequency from intermediate dose 28 dpi. CDRs boxed in grey; key mature (red) or on-track (dark blue; PCT64-only) mutations marked with letters. N at top left indicates number of sequences included in analysis. gl BG18 HC (top green); LMCA PCT64 HC (middle pink); CLK09 HC (bottom blue).</p>
<p id="P100">(<strong>J</strong>) Phylogenetic tree plots of each lineage after triple GT mRNA-LNP of intermediate dose 14 dpi. Each tree denotes a clonal lineage. Branch lengths are scaled by estimated heavy chain nt mutations. gl BG18 HC (top green); LMCA PCT64 HC (middle pink); CLK09 HC (bottom blue). Tree Scale (0.01) indicates the number of substitutions per site.</p></figcaption></figure><p id="P23">To distinguish the serum IgG contributions from WT CD45.1 and transferred CD45.2 B cells, ELISA analysis was performed from samples of untransferred, WT CD45.1 mice and CD45.2 adoptive transfer recipients primed with an intermediate dose of triple mRNA-LNPs. Higher IgG ΔAUC to N332-GT5 antigen was in CD45.2 recipients compared with untransferred WT mice; the same outcome was observed for ApexGT5 and eOD-GT8 specific-epitopes, although only at day 14 (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S11</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">G</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">I</a>). The IgG AUC to WT antigens varied with the epitope and time (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S11</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">J</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">L</a>); however, the overall IgG AUC to epitope-KO antigens was lower in CD45.2-transferred relative to WT recipients (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S11</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">M</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">O</a>), suggesting less off-target antibody production.</p>
<p id="P24">To determine whether SHM accumulated after triple mRNA delivery and whether mRNA dose affected maturation, CD45.2 epitope-specific B cell binders were collected and paired BCR sequences obtained via 10x Genomics. All precursor classes accumulated HC mutations over time at frequencies similar to those observed in dual activations, and immunogen dose did not affect mean nt and aa mutations for BG18 and PCT64 (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7</a>, <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">F</a> to <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">H</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S12</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">A</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">C</a>); CLK09 showed fewer mutations at 14 days for the intermediate dose relative to the low dose, but numbers reached equilibrium by day 28 (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7H</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S12C</a>). Mutational hotspots for BG18 and PCT64 HCs were also similar across doses (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7I</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S12</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">D</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">E</a>). CLK09 HC hotspot mutation analysis found high mutation rates in CDR3, and variation in CDR1 and 2 mutation patterns across doses, though this was likely due to low sequence recovery at day 28 (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7I</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S12F</a>). Phylogenetic analysis demonstrated that the heavy chains of each lineage diversified extensively post-prime (<a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Fig. 7J</a> and <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S12</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">G</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">I</a>). We then evaluated the neutralization ability of the IgGs purified from mouse serum after triple mRNA-LNP prime. No neutralization activity (IC50 &gt;50 μg/ml) against native or modified HIV pseudoviruses sensitive to mature BG18, PCT64 or VRC01 bnAb was detected. However, high dose day 28 serum showed some activity against BG505_V1<sub>GT5</sub> (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S13</a>). In sum, BG18-class/V3-glycan, PCT64-class/V2-apex, and VRC01-class/CD4bs-directed bnAb precursors can be activated simultaneously and undergo affinity maturation across a range of mRNA-LNP doses, diversifying extensively. Nonetheless, they require further SHM to neutralize native pseudovirus potently and broadly.</p></section><section id="S9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Precursors targeting four distinct epitopes can be activated simultaneously</h3>
<p id="P25">HIV bnAbs to the MPER, such as 10E8, exhibit particularly high neutralization breadth (<a href="#R63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R63">63</a>). The mouse model HuGL18<sup>H</sup> harbors the genuine human BCR HC of MPER-HuGL18 in association with endogenous murine LC; these BCRs can be activated by a 10E8-GT10.2 and GT10.3 12mer protein (for which MPER-HuGL18<sup>H</sup> BCRs had median affinities of ~80 nM) even at initial precursor frequencies (100:10<sup>6</sup>) approximately 11-fold lower than the naïve human repertoire (<a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">28</a>, <a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a>). To examine whether priming could further expand to encompass precursors to this epitope, we adoptively transferred CD45.2 BG18<sup>Hgl</sup> (7:10<sup>6</sup>), PCT64<sup>LMCA</sup> (10:10<sup>6</sup>), CLK09 (20:10<sup>6</sup>) and HuGL18<sup>H</sup> (100:10<sup>6</sup>) B cell precursors, and subsequently primed the recipients with the prior mRNA-LNP cocktail of N332-GT5, ApexGT5, and eOD-GT8 60mer, with the addition of mRNA-LNPs encoding a soluble 24mer called 10E8-GT12 (2 μg each). 10E8-GT12 has been further optimized from the 10E8-GT10 series for binding of both mature 10E8 and its precursors (<a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">28</a>) and binds the HuGL18 H/L pair with 33 nM affinity (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S14A</a>).</p>
<p id="P26">Samples were assayed 14 and 28 dpi (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8A</a>). Co-administration of the quadruple-GT mRNA-LNP cocktail induced strong GC reactions and a fraction of CD45.2<sup>+</sup> cells participating in GCs comparable to prior mRNA-LNP combinations (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>, <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">B</a> to <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">D</a>). CD45.2 epitope-specific binders targeting all antigens were prevalent at both time points, although the fraction of GC CD45.2 binders or CD45.2 binders in total B cells specific for 10E8-GT12 was lower than for the other three epitopes (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>, <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">E</a> and <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">F</a>). Serum IgG against each antigen was detected (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S14</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">B</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">I</a>), but the epitope-specific ΔAUC of N332-GT5 was, as in previous mixtures, highest (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>, <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">G</a> to <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">J</a>). Additionally, 10x BCR sequencing found that all lineages accumulated HC SHM over time (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8</a>, <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">K</a> to <a href="#F8" class="usa-link">R</a>), and BG18, PCT64 and CLK09 precursors acquired similar hotspot mutation patterns as observed in single or combination activations described above (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S14</a>, <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">J</a> to <a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">L</a>). HuGL18 exhibited high mutation rates in HCDRs (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S14M</a>), and all lineages underwent extensive diversification (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S14N</a>). Generally, sequences recovered at day 28 remained distant from the mature bnAbs, though key or on-track mutations were observed (<a href="#SD2" class="usa-link">fig. S15</a>). The addition of 10E8-class/MPER-directed bnAb precursors to the repertoire and a 10E8-GT immunogen to the cocktail did not inhibit the development of the other three lineages, suggesting that cocktails may be flexible as novel GT priming immunogens become available.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F8"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8. Simultaneous activation of four distinct classes of bnAb precursors.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12784461_nihms-2119667-f0008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/ab94/12784461/9ed5ac3d2603/nihms-2119667-f0008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1050" width="965" alt="Figure 8."></a></p>

ΔAUC comparison of titers for N332-GT5 and N332-GT5-KO (G); ApexGT5 and ApexGT5-KO (H) eOD-GT8 and eOD-GT8-KO (I); 10E8-GT12 and 10E8-GT12-KO (J).
